Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given two strings x and y, both consist only of lowercase Latin letters. Let |s| be the length of string s.

Let's call a sequence a a merging sequence if it consists of exactly |x| zeros and exactly |y| ones in some order.

A merge z is produced from a sequence a by the following rules: 

  * if a_i=0, then remove a letter from the beginning of x and append it to the end of z; 
  * if a_i=1, then remove a letter from the beginning of y and append it to the end of z. 



Two merging sequences a and b are different if there is some position i such that a_i ≠ b_i.

Let's call a string z chaotic if for all i from 2 to |z| z_{i-1} ≠ z_i.

Let s[l,r] for some 1 ≤ l ≤ r ≤ |s| be a substring of consecutive letters of s, starting from position l and ending at position r inclusive.

Let f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2) be the number of different merging sequences of x[l_1,r_1] and y[l_2,r_2] that produce chaotic merges. Note that only non-empty substrings of x and y are considered.

Calculate ∑ _{1 ≤ l_1 ≤ r_1 ≤ |x| \\\ 1 ≤ l_2 ≤ r_2 ≤ |y|} f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2). Output the answer modulo 998 244 353.

Input

The first line contains a string x (1 ≤ |x| ≤ 1000).

The second line contains a string y (1 ≤ |y| ≤ 1000).

Both strings consist only of lowercase Latin letters.

Output

Print a single integer — the sum of f(l_1, r_1, l_2, r_2) over 1 ≤ l_1 ≤ r_1 ≤ |x| and 1 ≤ l_2 ≤ r_2 ≤ |y| modulo 998 244 353.

Output

In the first example there are: 

  * 6 pairs of substrings "a" and "b", each with valid merging sequences "01" and "10"; 
  * 3 pairs of substrings "a" and "bb", each with a valid merging sequence "101"; 
  * 4 pairs of substrings "aa" and "b", each with a valid merging sequence "010"; 
  * 2 pairs of substrings "aa" and "bb", each with valid merging sequences "0101" and "1010"; 
  * 2 pairs of substrings "aaa" and "b", each with no valid merging sequences; 
  * 1 pair of substrings "aaa" and "bb" with a valid merging sequence "01010"; 



Thus, the answer is 6 ⋅ 2 + 3 ⋅ 1 + 4 ⋅ 1 + 2 ⋅ 2 + 2 ⋅ 0 + 1 ⋅ 1 = 24.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two strings x and y
2. We need to consider all possible substrings of x and y
3. For each pair of substrings (x[l1:r1], y[l2:r2]), we want to count the number of "merging sequences" that produce a "chaotic" merge
4. A merging sequence consists of 0s and 1s - 0 means take from start of x, 1 means take from start of y
5. A merge is chaotic if no two consecutive characters are the same
6. We need to sum f(l1,r1,l2,r2) over all possible substring pairs

## Key Insights

1. For a merge to be chaotic, no two consecutive characters in the result can be the same
2. This means we can't have "00" or "11" in consecutive positions
3. The merging sequence alternates between taking from x and y
4. The final merge will be a string where we take characters from x and y in alternating fashion

## Approach

For each substring of x and each substring of y, we need to:
1. Check if there exists any valid merging sequence that produces a chaotic merge
2. Count how many such sequences exist

However, a simpler observation: for a merge to be chaotic, we need to alternate between taking from x and y. This means:
- If we take from x at position i, the next must be from y
- If we take from y at position i, the next must be from x

So we can use dynamic programming to count valid sequences.

## Solution Strategy

For each pair of substrings (x[l1:r1], y[l2:r2]), we can use DP where:
- dp[i][j][last] = number of ways to process first i characters of x[l1:r1] and first j characters of y[l2:r2], ending with 'last' (0 or 1)

But this would be too slow. Let's optimize:

Actually, we can notice that a merge is chaotic if and only if we alternate between x and y, and the resulting characters don't create consecutive identical characters. Since we're just appending characters, the only constraint is that we can't have two consecutive same characters.

## Optimized Approach

For a given pair of substrings (x[l1:r1], y[l2:r2]):
- We need to consider all possible ways to interleave characters from x and y
- The resulting string must not have consecutive identical characters
- We can use DP: dp[i][j][prev] = number of ways to take i characters from x and j characters from y, ending with prev (0 for x, 1 for y)

Base case: dp[0][0][None] = 1
Transition:
- dp[i][j][0] += dp[i-1][j][prev] if prev != 0 (to ensure alternation)
- dp[i][j][1] += dp[i][j-1][prev] if prev != 1 (to ensure alternation)

Wait, I need to reconsider. The constraint is that the merge is chaotic, meaning consecutive characters in the final string are different. Since we're taking characters from x and y in order, the final string is built by appending characters.

Actually, let me think simpler:
- We build the merge by taking characters from x or y in sequence
- We need to ensure that no two consecutive characters in the result are the same
- We can use DP where the state includes the current position in x, position in y, and the last character taken

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 998244353
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    total = 0
    
    # For each substring of x
    for l1 in range(n):
        for r1 in range(l1, n):
            # For each substring of y
            for l2 in range(m):
                for r2 in range(l2, m):
                    # Count valid merging sequences for substrings x[l1:r1+1] and y[l2:r2+1]
                    substr_x = x[l1:r1+1]
                    substr_y = y[l2:r2+1]
                    
                    # DP: dp[i][j][last_char] = number of ways to process i chars from substr_x, j chars from substr_y
                    # where last_char is 0 (from x) or 1 (from y) or -1 (start)
                    dp = [[[0, 0] for _ in range(len(substr_y) + 1)] for _ in range(len(substr_x) + 1)]
                    
                    # Base case: empty sequences
                    dp[0][0][1] = 1  # meaning we haven't taken any character yet
                    
                    # Fill DP table
                    for i in range(len(substr_x) + 1):
                        for j in range(len(substr_y) + 1):
                            if i == 0 and j == 0:
                                continue
                                
                            # If we can extend by taking from x
                            if i > 0:
                                # We came from (i-1, j)
                                prev_last = 1 if j > 0 else -1
                                # For the current character from x
                                if j == 0 or substr_x[i-1] != substr_y[j-1] or (j > 0 and substr_y[j-1] != substr_x[i-1]): 
                                    # This condition is wrong, let me fix
                                    pass
                            
                    # Actually, let's restart with a simpler correct DP
                    # We need to ensure the merge is chaotic, so no consecutive same characters
                    
                    # Correct DP approach
                    dp = [[[0 for _ in range(2)] for _ in range(len(substr_y) + 1)] for _ in range(len(substr_x) + 1)]
                    
                    # dp[i][j][0] = number of ways to process i chars from x, j chars from y, ending with x
                    # dp[i][j][1] = number of ways to process i chars from x, j chars from y, ending with y
                    
                    # Base case: at start, no character taken
                    dp[0][0][0] = 1  # we can start with either
                    dp[0][0][1] = 1
                    
                    # Actually, we need to be careful about the start
                    
                    # Let's set dp[0][0][0] = 1, dp[0][0][1] = 1 (valid empty state)
                    # But we need to handle the first character
                    
                    # Actually, let's do it differently
                    dp = [[0] * 2 for _ in range(len(substr_y) + 1)]
                    # dp[j][prev] = number of ways to process first i chars of x and first j chars of y, ending with prev (0=x, 1=y)
                    
                    # Start with empty
                    result = 0
                    
                    # We'll do a clean DP
                    if len(substr_x) == 0 and len(substr_y) == 0:
                        result = 1  # empty merge is chaotic (vacuously true)
                    elif len(substr_x) == 0:
                        # Only from y
                        if len(substr_y) == 0:
                            result = 1
                        else:
                            # All characters must be different from previous
                            # Since we take from y continuously, we need to check if all chars are distinct
                            # But they have to alternate, which isn't possible since we only take from y
                            # So if we take from y only, we have consecutive duplicates
                            result = 0
                    elif len(substr_y) == 0:
                        # Only from x
                        if len(substr_x) == 0:
                            result = 1
                        else:
                            result = 0
                    else:
                        # Both non-empty
                        # Use DP with state (i, j, last_char)
                        dp = [[[0, 0] for _ in range(len(substr_y) + 1)] for _ in range(len(substr_x) + 1)]
                        dp[0][0][0] = 1
                        dp[0][0][1] = 1
                        
                        for i in range(len(substr_x) + 1):
                            for j in range(len(substr_y) + 1):
                                if i == 0 and j == 0:
                                    continue
                                    
                                # If we can take from x
                                if i > 0:
                                    # We came from (i-1, j)
                                    # The previous character is in dp[i-1][j][prev]
                                    for prev in range(2):
                                        if dp[i-1][j][prev] > 0:
                                            # We're taking from x, so new char is x[i-1]
                                            # We need to ensure it's different from previous
                                            if prev == 0:  # previous was from x
                                                # Can't take from x again
                                                continue
                                            elif prev == 1:  # previous was from y
                                                # Can take from x as long as it's different from y's last char
                                                # But we don't have information about y's last char
                                                # This approach is flawed
                                                pass
                        
                        # Actually, I need to track the last character taken
                        # Let me restructure
                        
                        # dp[i][j][0] = ways to reach (i,j) ending with character from x
                        # dp[i][j][1] = ways to reach (i,j) ending with character from y
                        
                        dp = [[[0, 0] for _ in range(len(substr_y) + 1)] for _ in range(len(substr_x) + 1)]
                        
                        # dp[0][0] is undefined, so start from valid states
                        # For (0,0): no characters taken, so we can start with either
                        # But for non-empty sequences, we need to start with one character
                        
                        dp[0][0][0] = 1  # starting with no character
                        dp[0][0][1] = 1
                        
                        # Process all positions
                        for i in range(len(substr_x) + 1):
                            for j in range(len(substr_y) + 1):
                                # Skip if we're at start
                                if i == 0 and j == 0:
                                    continue
                                    
                                # If we can take from x
                                if i > 0:
                                    # We come from (i-1, j)
                                    for last in range(2):
                                        if dp[i-1][j][last] > 0:
                                            # We take from x at position i-1
                                            # If last == 0 (previous was from x), we can't take from x again
                                            if last == 0:
                                                continue
                                            # Otherwise we can take from x
                                            # The new character is x[i-1], we need to ensure it's different from the previous character in the result
                                            # But we don't know the actual character, only that it came from x or y
                                            # Actually, we need to know what the last character was
                                            
                                            # If last == 0, we came from x, so we can't take from x again
                                            # If last == 1, we came from y, so we can take from x
                                            
                                            # We need to ensure that the character we take is different from the last character
                                            # But we don't have the actual character, only the source
                                            
                                            # This approach won't work because we lose character information
                                            
                                            pass
                    
                    # I think I need to track both source and character
                    # dp[i][j][0][c] = number of ways with last being from x with character c
                    # This will be too large
                    
                    # Let me try a different insight
                    
                    # For the merge to be chaotic, since we alternate between x and y, we need that:
                    # - For each consecutive pair, the characters are different
                    # - This happens when we alternate sources, and the characters at those positions are different
                    
                    # Actually, let me look at the pattern:
                    # The merge alternates between taking from x and y
                    # So the sequence is: (x[i], y[j], x[k], y[l], ...)
                    
                    # For chaos: x[i] != y[j], y[j] != x[k], x[k] != y[l], etc.
                    
                    # But this isn't quite right - the characters are appended, not paired
                    
                    # The merge string is: c1, c2, c3, ...
                    # We need c1 != c2, c2 != c3, c3 != c4, etc.
                    
                    # If we take from x at step t, then the next must be from y, and the character from x must differ from the character from y
                    # Similarly, if we take from y first, then from x, the characters must differ
                    
                    # Actually, the constraint is only on consecutive characters in the result, not on the sources
                    
                    # So we need a DP that tracks:
                    # i, j, and the last character (which is the character that was added to the result)
                    
                    # dp[i][j][c] = number of ways to process first i characters of x and first j characters of y, with the last character being c
                    
                    # But we need to know the last character, not just its source
                    
                    # We can do: 
                    # dp[i][j][c] = number of ways where the last character added is c
                    
                    # Transition:
                    # 1. If we can take from x: if i > 0, then we can add x[i-1] to the sequence
                    #    if the last character was not x[i-1], then this is valid
                    # 2. If we can take from y: if j > 0, then we can add y[j-1] to the sequence
                    #    if the last character was not y[j-1], then this is valid
                    
                    # Base case: at start, we haven't added any character
                    # So we need to initialize for first character
                    
                    # For first character:
                    # If we take from x: we add x[0], so dp[1][0][x[0]] += 1
                    # If we take from y: we add y[0], so dp[0][1][y[0]] += 1
                    
                    # Then for subsequent steps:
                    # dp[i][j][c] += dp[i-1][j][prev] if c != prev and we took from x
                    # dp[i][j][c] += dp[i][j-1][prev] if c != prev and we took from y
                    
                    # We need to handle the case where both i and j are 0
                    
                    # Let's implement this
                    
                    if len(substr_x) == 0 and len(substr_y) == 0:
                        result = 1
                    elif len(substr_x) == 0:
                        # Only from y
                        result = 0  # consecutive characters from y are the same
                    elif len(substr_y) == 0:
                        # Only from x
                        result = 0  # consecutive characters from x are the same
                    else:
                        # DP: dp[i][j][c] where c is the last character added
                        # Use dictionary or array for characters
                        dp = [[[0] * 26 for _ in range(len(substr_y) + 1)] for _ in range(len(substr_x) + 1)]
                        
                        # Base case for first character
                        if len(substr_x) > 0:
                            dp[1][0][ord(substr_x[0]) - ord('a')] = 1
                        if len(substr_y) > 0:
                            dp[0][1][ord(substr_y[0]) - ord('a')] = 1
                            
                        # Fill dp table
                        for i in range(len(substr_x) + 1):
                            for j in range(len(substr_y) + 1):
                                # Skip if both are 0, but we already handled that
                                if i == 0 and j == 0:
                                    continue
                                    
                                # If we can go to (i+1, j)
                                if i < len(substr_x):
                                    # Take from x at position i
                                    char_x = ord(substr_x[i]) - ord('a')
                                    for prev_char in range(26):
                                        if dp[i][j][prev_char] > 0:
                                            # We can take from x only if the last character is not the same
                                            if prev_char != char_x:
                                                dp[i+1][j][char_x] = (dp[i+1][j][char_x] + dp[i][j][prev_char]) % MOD
                                
                                # If we can go to (i, j+1)
                                if j < len(substr_y):
                                    char_y = ord(substr_y[j]) - ord('a')
                                    for prev_char in range(26):
                                        if dp[i][j][prev_char] > 0:
                                            if prev_char != char_y:
                                                dp[i][j+1][char_y] = (dp[i][j+1][char_y] + dp[i][j][prev_char]) % MOD
                        
                        # Sum all final states
                        result = 0
                        for c in range(26):
                            result = (result + dp[len(substr_x)][len(substr_y)][c]) % MOD
                    
                    total = (total + result) % MOD
    
    print(total)

solve()
```

Wait, I think there's a bug in my DP initialization. Let me fix it:

```python
MOD = 998244353

def solve():
    x = input().strip()
    y = input().strip()
    
    n, m = len(x), len(y)
    total = 0
    
    # For each substring of x
    for l1 in range(n):
        for r1 in range(l1, n):
            substr_x = x[l1:r1+1]
            for l2 in range(m):
                for r2 in range(l2, m):
                    substr_y = y[l2:r2+1]
                    
                    # Count valid merging sequences for this pair
                    if len(substr_x) == 0 and len(substr_y) == 0:
                        # Empty merge is chaotic
                        total = (total + 1) % MOD
                    elif len(substr_x) == 0:
                        # Only from y, can't have chaotic merge (consecutive same characters)
                        # unless length is 0
                        total = (total + 0) % MOD
                    elif len(substr_y) == 0:
                        # Only from x, can't have chaotic merge
                        total = (total + 0) % MOD
                    else:
                        # Use DP with state (i, j, last_char)
                        # dp[i][j][c] = number of ways to take i from x, j from y, ending with character c
                        # c is from 'a' to 'z'
                        
                        # Initialize dp array
                        dp = [[[0] * 26 for _ in range(len(substr_y) + 1)] for _ in range(len(substr_x) + 1)]
                        
                        # Base case: first character
                        # If we take from x
                        if len(substr_x) > 0:
                            dp[1][0][ord(substr_x[0]) - ord('a')] = 1
                        # If we take from y
                        if len(substr_y) > 0:
                            dp[0][1][ord(substr_y[0]) - ord('a')] = 1
                        
                        # Fill DP table
                        for i in range(len(substr_x) + 1):
                            for j in range(len(substr_y) + 1):
                                # Skip if both are 0
                                if i == 0 and j == 0:
                                    continue
                                    
                                # If we can take from x (i < len(substr_x))
                                if i < len(substr_x):
                                    char_x = ord(substr_x[i]) - ord('a')
                                    for prev_char in range(26):
                                        if dp[i][j][prev_char] > 0:
                                            # Can take from x only if the previous character is different
                                            if prev_char != char_x:
                                                dp[i+1][j][char_x] = (dp[i+1][j][char_x] + dp[i][j][prev_char]) % MOD
                                
                                # If we can take from y (j < len(substr_y))
                                if j < len(substr_y):
                                    char_y = ord(substr_y[j]) - ord('a')
                                    for prev_char in range(26):
                                        if dp[i][j][prev_char] > 0:
                                            if prev_char != char_y:
                                                dp[i][j+1][char_y] = (dp[i][j+1][char_y] + dp[i][j][prev_char]) % MOD
                        
                        # Sum all ways to reach the end
                        result = 0
                        for c in range(26):
                            result = (result + dp[len(substr_x)][len(substr_y)][c]) % MOD
                        total = (total + result) % MOD
    
    print(total)

solve()
```

This should work. Let me trace through the first example:
- x="aaa", y="bb"
- We need to check all substring pairs

The key insight is that for a merge to be chaotic, consecutive characters in the result must be different. My DP correctly tracks this by ensuring that when we add a character, it's different from the previous character in the result.

```python
MOD = 998244353

x = input().strip()
y = input().strip()

n, m = len(x), len(y)
total = 0

# For each substring of x
for l1 in range(n):
    for r1 in range(l1, n):
        substr_x = x[l1:r1+1]
        for l2 in range(m):
            for r2 in range(l2, m):
                substr_y = y[l2:r2+1]
                
                # Count valid merging sequences for this pair
                if len(substr_x) == 0 and len(substr_y) == 0:
                    # Empty merge is chaotic
                    total = (total + 1) % MOD
                elif len(substr_x) == 0:
                    # Only from y, can't have chaotic merge (consecutive same characters)
                    total = (total + 0) % MOD
                elif len(substr_y) == 0:
                    # Only from x, can't have chaotic merge
                    total = (total + 0) % MOD
                else:
                    # Use DP with state (i, j, last_char)
                    # dp[i][j][c] = number of ways to take i from x, j from y, ending with character c
                    # c is from 'a' to 'z'
                    
                    # Initialize dp array
                    dp = [[[0] * 26 for _ in range(len(substr_y) + 1)] for _ in range(len(substr_x) + 1)]
                    
                    # Base case: first character
                    # If we take from x
                    if len(substr_x) > 0:
                        dp[1][0][ord(substr_x[0]) - ord('a')] = 1
                    # If we take from y
                    if len(substr_y) > 0:
                        dp[0][1][ord(substr_y[0]) - ord('a')] = 1
                    
                    # Fill DP table
                    for i in range(len(substr_x) + 1):
                        for j in range(len(substr_y) + 1):
                            # Skip if both are 0
                            if i == 0 and j == 0:
                                continue
                            
                            # If we can take from x (i < len(substr_x))
                            if i < len(substr_x):
                                char_x = ord(substr_x[i]) - ord('a')
                                for prev_char in range(26):
                                    if dp[i][j][prev_char] > 0:
                                        # Can take from x only if the previous character is different
                                        if prev_char != char_x:
                                            dp[i+1][j][char_x] = (dp[i+1][j][char_x] + dp[i][j][prev_char]) % MOD
                            
                            # If we can take from y (j < len(substr_y))
                            if j < len(substr_y):
                                char_y = ord(substr_y[j]) - ord('a')
                                for prev_char in range(26):
                                    if dp[i][j][prev_char] > 0:
                                        if prev_char != char_y:
                                            dp[i][j+1][char_y] = (dp[i][j+1][char_y] + dp[i][j][prev_char]) % MOD
                    
                    # Sum all ways to reach the end
                    result = 0
                    for c in range(26):
                        result = (result + dp[len(substr_x)][len(substr_y)][c]) % MOD
                    total = (total + result) % MOD

print(total)
```
